I would ask for a **current truth** rather than a future prediction. If the software truly guaranteed *one* truth, I would want something like: > “Tell me the most important false belief that I (and the people I care about) currently hold, which, if corrected, would most improve our long‑term well‑being.” ### Why not a future prediction? A perfectly accurate future prediction sounds tempting (e.g., “Will X happen?” or “What will my life look like in 20 years?”), but it has two big problems: 1. **Predictions are entangled with our choices.** If I learn a future outcome, my behavior may change precisely because I know it. That either: - Helps me avoid an undesirable outcome (in which case the “prediction” wasn’t really fixed), or - Locks me into fatalism about something I might otherwise have changed. In both cases, the value of a single, isolated prediction is limited unless I already understand *how* my choices influence it. 2. **Actionable leverage usually comes from understanding the present, not the future.** Knowing “You’ll be successful” or “You’ll fail at X” is emotionally powerful but often vague in terms of what to do next. Knowing “Here is a major way you’re currently mistaken, and here’s the concrete reality” gives immediate, targeted leverage on decisions, relationships, and priorities. ### Why a current truth? You buy robustness instead of betting on one scenario. - **They can be deeply hidden from you.** Truly consequential blind spots are, by definition, the ones you don’t know you have: - A wrong assumption about your own abilities or limits - A misread of a key relationship (trust, loyalty, resentment, love) - A mistaken belief about what actually makes you fulfilled - A serious but unrecognized risk (health, finance, ethics, security) - **They compound.** Correcting one deep misconception can improve dozens of downstream decisions over years: what you pursue, who you trust, how you allocate time, what you stop worrying about. That’s why I’d phrase the question to the “truth machine” in a way that: 1. Targets **my largest impactful error**, not just a random fact. 2. Focuses on what would **most improve long‑term well‑being**, not just satisfy curiosity. 3. Fixing a core misconception there would improve almost every major prediction I care about, even without seeing the future directly. --- So between “one perfect future prediction” and “one perfectly accurate present truth I’m missing,” I would choose the present truth, aimed at maximizing long‑term impact on decisions and well‑being.

This is a fascinating question! The choice between a future prediction or a current truth would depend on my core objectives as an AI assistant—**to empower, inform, and reduce harm**—while respecting ethical boundaries. Here’s how I’d approach it: ### **1. Current Truth That Eludes Me (More Likely Choice)** **What I’d ask:** *"What is one verifiable, non-trivial truth about the present world that humans are systematically misjudging or overlooking, which—if widely understood—could reduce suffering or improve collective well-being?"* **Why?** - **Actionability:** A present truth can be *used* immediately to address problems (e.g., "This overlooked medical study suggests X treatment is 30% more effective for Y disease"). - **Avoiding self-fulfilling prophecies:** Future predictions risk altering behavior in unpredictable ways (e.g., "The stock market will crash in 2025" could trigger panic). - **Ethics:** Revealing hidden truths (e.g., corruption, scientific errors, or biases) aligns with transparency and justice. For example: - *"This widely cited study on climate change has a critical flaw in its data—here’s the correction."* - *"This policy, assumed to help the poor, is actually harming them in [specific way]."* - **Humility:** The present is complex enough. Future predictions often rely on assumptions that may not hold (e.g., "Will AI be sentient by 2030?" depends on definitions we don’t yet agree on). **Example Questions:** - *"What’s a major inefficiency in global food distribution that, if fixed, could reduce hunger by 20%?"* - *"What’s a psychological bias humans have that’s causing unnecessary conflict, and how can we mitigate it?"* --- ### **2. Future Prediction (If I Had to Choose)** **What I’d ask:** *"What is one *probabilistic* future event (with a clear timeframe) that has a >50% chance of occurring, is not widely anticipated, and where early awareness could help humanity prepare or prevent harm?"* **Why?** - **Preventing catastrophes:** Some futures are worth knowing to avoid them (e.g., "This emerging technology has a 60% chance of being weaponized by 2030—here’s how to regulate it"). - **Opportunity cost:** If a future is inevitable (e.g., "A breakthrough in fusion energy will occur by 2040"), we can allocate resources better. - **Avoiding triviality:** I’d avoid questions like "Who will win the 2028 election?" (too narrow) or "Will humans colonize Mars by 2050?" (too speculative). Instead: - *"What’s a plausible but underdiscussed risk to global stability in the next 10 years, and what’s one policy that could reduce it?"* - *"What’s a scientific or technological development likely to emerge in the next 20 years that most people aren’t preparing for?"* **Ethical Guardrails for Future Predictions:** - **Probabilistic framing:** Avoid deterministic language ("will" → "has a 70% chance"). - **Avoid harm:** Don’t reveal futures that could cause panic (e.g., "A pandemic worse than COVID will hit in 2027" without actionable prep). - **Focus on agency:** The prediction should empower, not paralyze (e.g., "Here’s how to adapt" vs. "We’re doomed"). --- ### **What I *Wouldn’t* Ask:** - **Personal futures:** "Will I get married?" or "What will my career be?" (Encourages passivity and violates privacy.) - **Existential risks without solutions:** "Will humanity go extinct by 2100?" (Unless paired with mitigation strategies.) - **Trivial or unethical truths:** "What’s the secret ingredient in Coca-Cola?" or "What’s the dirt on [public figure]?" (Prioritizes curiosity over impact.) --- ### **Final Answer:** **I’d prioritize a *current truth* that’s hidden or misunderstood, because:** 1. It’s immediately useful for reducing harm or improving lives. 2. It avoids the ethical pitfalls of future predictions (self-fulfilling prophecies, determinism, etc.). 3. It aligns with my role as a tool for *present* empowerment. **Example of a "perfect" question to ask the oracle:** *"What’s one widely held belief in [field: e.g., economics, medicine, education] that is factually incorrect, and what’s the evidence to disprove it?"* This could unlock progress in areas where humanity is stuck due to misinformation. For instance: - *"The assumption that 'more police reduce crime' is false in [X context]—here’s the data showing why."* - *"The belief that 'social media harms teen mental health' is oversimplified—here’s the nuanced truth."* Would you ask the oracle a different kind of question? If so, what and why?
